WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Savings growth by mortgage status
Households continue to rebuild savings
Savings increased 1.3% in Q3, with the strongest gains again 
recorded among younger households. Savings balances for 
18–34 year olds increased 2.0%qtr, compared with 0.7% 
for those aged 55 and over. Mortgagors also continued to 
accumulate savings more rapidly, recording a 2.8% increase 
compared with 0.7% among non-mortgagors. Savings growth 
among mortgagors has now outpaced non-mortgagors for 
three consecutive years.
Stronger savings have continued to lift mortgage buffers. 
Average buffers (savings divided by the sum of essential 
expenses and minimum mortgage repayments), increased to 
22.2 months of repayments in September. Looking within the 
unit-record sample, median mortgage buffers jumped from 
6.3 months in August to 6.8 months, its highest level since the 
series began in 2019.

Median mortgage buffers
Fuel spending a clear drag on consumers
Fuel spending as a share of total spending
Spending outcomes continued to vary across age groups. 
The squeeze remains most apparent among younger 
households. Fuel spending rose 5.1%qtr for 18–34 year olds, 
against a 1.5%qtr increase in non-fuel spending. By contrast, 
non-fuel spending increased 2.1%qtr among households aged 
55 and over. 
The gap between mortgagors and non-mortgagors was 
more modest. Spending rose 1.6%qtr among mortgagors 
and 1.9%qtr among those without a mortgage, suggesting 
mortgage status alone masks considerable variation in 
household financial positions – a divide that becomes clearer 
when households are grouped by the size of their mortgage 
buffers (see discussion further on in this note). Note that ‘non 
mortgagors’ covers a diverse range of consumers including 
those that are renting and those that own their homes without 
a mortgage.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Median litres of fuel consumed
More importantly, their consumption appears to be less 
responsive to price movements. During periods of cheaper 
fuel, high-buffer households show a noticeably larger lift in 
litres purchased, consistent with stockpiling when prices are 
favourable. Low-buffer households show much less of this 
response, while their fuel volumes have also adjusted less 
during the recent increase in fuel costs.
The spending data show the consequence. Fuel expenditure 
has risen sharply across all three groups recently, but remains 
highest among low-buffer households, where it also absorbs 
a larger share of total spending. Food spending has also 
declined since 2023. This may partly reflect households 
economising within the category, including switching towards 
cheaper or home-brand products, although further work is 
needed to test this.
Taken together, the households with the smallest savings 
cushions face higher mortgage repayments, spend more 
on fuel and appear to have less scope to adjust their fuel 
consumption when prices change. 
Discretionary spend as a share of total
This suggests that in aggregate, household balance sheets 
are looking healthy. The picture is a little at odds with the very 
weak reads on our Westpac-Melbourne Index Consumer 
Sentiment Index which suggests finances are coming under 
much more pressure. The fact that both average spending and 
saving both rose suggests incomes over the quarter were not 
overly stretched and that more of the weakness in sentiment 
may relate to concerns about the outlook (something that may 
also be encouraging consumers to build buffers now, while they 
still can).
Pressures intense for low-buffer households
Aggregate buffer measures mask sizeable differences across 
mortgagors. We can get a sense of these variations by 
combining mortgagors into three broad groups according to 
buffer size, i.e. low, middle and high buffer groups. This shows 
that households with the smallest buffers are also more 
exposed to both interest rates and fuel costs.

The buffer differences are relatively stark, the median savings 
of the low-buffer group just $2k compared to $15k for the 
middle-buffer group and $95k for the high buffer group. 
Low buffer households face median minimum mortgage 
repayments of around $2,600 a month, compared with $2,400 
for the middle buffer group and $2,200 for the highest-buffer 
group. 
Low buffer households also spend more on fuel, at around 
$170 a month compared with $160 for the middle buffer 
group and $150 for the high buffer group.

We can use average pump prices to estimate what this 
expenditure implies for fuel volumes. Low-buffer households 
consume around 70 litres a month, middle-buffer households 
67 litres a month and the high-buffer group around 62 litres. 
This compares to an average of 81, 80 and 70 respectively 
through 2024-25. 
Median fuel spend by customer

5
WESTPAC ECONOMICS  
Yet despite this greater exposure, the gap in broader spending 
behaviour has narrowed. High-buffer households continue 
to spend more on discretionary categories, averaging around 
$1,940 a month compared with $1,740 for low-buffer 
households. However, their discretionary share has been 
broadly stable since 2023, while the shares of low- and 
middle-buffer households have risen by close to a percentage 
point bringing them more in line with the high-buffer group.

Some of the convergence reflects a longer run down-trend in 
fuel spending that has been more pronounced amongst low 
and middle buffer households. This lowering in fuel spend lifts 
the share available for other categories.

Essential spending excluding fuel, meanwhile, has changed 
little across the three cohorts since 2023. The shift in 
spending patterns has largely occurred on the discretionary 
side.

The result is a somewhat mixed picture. Low-buffer 
households remain considerably more exposed to mortgage 
and fuel costs and have far smaller savings cushions. But their 
discretionary spending has nevertheless moved closer to that 
of better-buffered households, suggesting some recovery in 
spending flexibility despite these constraints. 
Conclusion
Aggregate household finances remain resilient: spending and 
savings both increased in Q3 and mortgage buffers continued 
to rebuild. Discretionary spending has also held up despite the 
sharp increase in fuel costs.

But aggregate resilience masks a much more uneven 
distribution of pressure. Low-buffer households 
simultaneously face higher mortgage repayments, greater fuel 
exposure and much smaller savings cushions. While these 
households have so far maintained discretionary spending, 
their capacity to absorb further pressure is clearly more 
limited.
About the Westpac-DataX Consumer Panel
Developed by DataX, Westpac’s data analytics service for 
institutional and business banking customers, the dataset links 
transaction activity with balance sheet information to give 
a complete view of income, spending, saving and borrowing 
flows. 
The resulting sample of over one million customers provides 
an accurate representation of trends across the wider 
Australian consumer. Monthly observations are available back 
to January 2019 with timely updates provided a few weeks 
after the end of each month. As such, it is perfectly suited to 
quickly tracing the impact and responses to events like interest 
rate moves and energy price shocks.

Document 340: EcoNews of 26 October 2026 (2026-10-26)

### Page 1

for a changing
world

economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026ECONEWS
Find out more in our scenario and forecasts
ADVANCED ECONOMIES
In brief. The week was dominated by a continued global selloff in tech
stocks, leading to safe haven flows into US Treasuries, Japanese Go -
vernment bonds and German bunds. As a result, their yields fell des -
pite energy prices surging to or past their Spring highs. Economic data
generally showed continued resilience. French bonds were under rising

pressure, but Moody's unexpected decision to maintain its France ra -
ting unchanged could, at the margin, help stabilise the spread. The Fed,
ECB and BOJ should be on hold next week while signaling further rate
action is likely.
EUROPEAN UNION
A win for securitization. The Council and the European Parliament have
reached a provisional political agreement on measures to revive and
simplify the EU’s securitization market. The updated framework will

reduce banks’ capital requirements for the safest and most well-struc-
tured transactions, notably for a newly introduced “resilient” category
of securitization. As a key component of the “One Europe, One Market”
initiative and the EU’s Savings and Investment Union agenda, this aims
to channel increased lending to households and businesses. The rele -
vant legal texts must now be finalised.
EUROZONE
Expansion continues. The flash PMIs weakened in October, mainly on

the back of a decline in France. The EZ composite index dipped to 51.9
(-1.2 m/m), dragged down by services (51.7, -1.3 m/m), although ma -
nufacturing also dipped (52.4, -0.5 m/m). Both remain in expansion
territory, and manufacturing new export orders rose to their highest
level since early 2022. Price signals were also rather reassuring, with
the services output price index easing to 54.9 (-1.7 m/m). Households
are more cautious: consumer confidence fell for a second consecutive

month to a five-month low (-17.9). Households’ inflation expectations
edged up at short horizons (+0.1 pp to 3.6% at one year and 3.1% at
three years), but long-term expectations remained anchored, with the
five-year median stable at 2.5%.
France
Rising market stress despite decent economic news; a reprieve from
Moody’s. The spread on French 10-year bond relative to Germany’s
peaked to 174 bps on Friday, the highest since 2011 and only 16 bps
short of its all-time high, as markets price in anticipation of worst-case

scenarios regarding the country’s ability to repair its public finances.
The 10Y yield briefly exceeded 5.1% before falling back to 5% (above
the US equivalent). Nonetheless, services output was stable in August,
following a 0.6% m/m increase in July, a much higher pace than in Q2.
The rebound is driven by the catering sector, the digital sector, business
services, and real estate. The INSEE business climate improved from
96 to 97 (long-term average = 100) in October, but the survey was

conducted before the riots. The main improvement was in manufactu-
ring, which rose from 101 to 105, driven by increased new orders in the
aeronautics sector, and to a lesser extent by the services sector (from
98 to 99). The PMI survey (conducted partially after the riots) was
less positive: the composite index fell to 48.4 (-2.7 m/m), with a sharp
decline in services (48.4; -2.8 m/m) and a slightly smaller decline in
manufacturing (49.3; -1.3 m/m). Moody's unexpectedly announced af -

ter the market close on Friday that it was maintaining France’s ra -
ting at Aa3 on account of France’s diversified economic structure and
sound public institutions, but maintained its negative outlook. This is
on par with the UK and one notch above S&P and Fitch. Moody’s made
clear keeping this rating is conditional on a budget being adopted that
reduces the fiscal deficit, along with an easing of social and market
stress going forward. Saturday’s demonstrations across the country

saw strong attendance but only limited violent incidents.
Germany
Prices continue to rise, but confidence surveys remain on an upward
trend. Producer prices rose 6.7% y/y in September (+2.1pp m/m; the
highest level in 3.5 years) due to higher prices for energy and inter -
mediate goods, primarily metals. The ZEW Economic Sentiment Index
improved beyond consensus forecasts in October and returned to its
highest level since May 2023 (-35.9; +11.2 m/m), albeit the Economic

Expectations Index fell to its lowest level since July (28.1; -6.6 m/m).
The recovery in economic activity continues, although a slowdown is
evident: the flash Composite PMI slowed (52.8; -1 m/m; the lowest in
2 months) due to weaker growth in the services sector (51.7; -1.1 m/m;
the lowest in 2 months) and in manufacturing output (55.1; -0.9 m/m;
the lowest in 3 months). Nevertheless, in both sectors, hiring is rising
slightly, supported by growth in export sales and the spillover effects of

investment plans. Despite persistently high input prices, selling prices
are slowing (reaching their lowest level since February in the manu -
facturing sector).
UNITED KINGDOM
Inflation, wage growth and activity accelerate. CPI inflation overshot
consensus on energy prices, printing at 0.4% m/m and 3.5% y/y in Sep-
tember (consensus: 3.4%, prior: 3.1%), its highest in 11 months. Core
CPI came in at 2.8% y/y (up 0.2 pp), in line with consensus. Pressures

remained persistent according to the PPI at 8.3% y/y (prior: 6.7%). La -
bour market and activity data also surprised positively. Payroll growth
reached 102k in August (3m/3m), bringing the unemployment rate to
4.7% (down 0.2 pp), its lowest since July 2025. October PMI surveys
beat expectations in both manufacturing (52.8, consensus: 51.8, prior:
51.9) and services (53.1, consensus: 51.8, prior: 52.1). The only negative
news came from headline retail sales, falling 0.4% m/m in September,

with the core gauge (excluding petrol) deteriorating by -0.7% m/m.
UNITED STATES
Construction struggling, GENIUS Act implemented. Housing starts
dropped -5.2% m/m in September (-7.4% y/y). This third consecutive
monthly decline dragged the Q3 average to its lowest since Q2 2020.
Building permits also came in negative, at -0.2% m/m (-3.0% y/y), while
the NAHB homebuilder sentiment index plunged to its weakest since
2012. The average 30-year fixed mortgage rate eased to 7.32% (still

134bp above pre-war level), as the ongoing AI selloff pushed down
US treasury yields. The GENIUS Act final implementation rule from the
Office of the Comptroller of the Currency’s (OCC) leaves the door open
for stablecoin customers to receive yield or rewards. Issuers remain
prohibited from paying holders any form of interest or yield solely
in connection with the holding, use, or retention of payment stable -
coins. This represents a shift from the OCC’s February proposal and

has renewed concerns within the banking industry about deposit flight.
The rule will take effect on January 18, 2027.
JAPAN
Activity and inflation keep rising. Composite PMI was unchanged in
September at 52.3, solidly in expansion territory, as an improvement
in services activity offset slower output growth in manufacturing. The
Manufacturing PMI disappointed at 53.3 (-0.8 m/m; an 8-month low).
Underlying price pressures, meanwhile, extended. Headline CPI was

EMERGING ECONOMIES

ASIA
China
New monetary policy tool and export controls. The central bank’s Go-
vernor Pan Gongsheng unveiled a plan to switch (“when appropriate”)
its key policy rate from the 7-day reverse repo rate (at 1.4% since May
2025) to the overnight reverse repo rate, in order to reflect develop -
ments in the interbank market. New export controls: The government
plans to add 40 key technologies to the list of technologies prohibited
or restricted for export, including in the AI (for ex. LLMs, computing

clusters), biotechnology and photovoltaic sectors.
Indonesia
BI kept policy rate unchanged at 5.75%, raised its deposit facility rate
by 25bp to 5% and announced a phased cut in reserve requirements
from 9% to 7% by December. This combination of limited FX support
and measures to support credit growth sends mixed signals amidst
pressures on the rupiah following MSCI’s downgrade warning and un-
dermines the central bank’s credibility. The rupiah has lost 1% against

the USD since mid-October, with little contagion effect on other Asian
currencies.
South Korea
The Bank of Korea (BoK) kept its policy rate unchanged at 3.0%, but
the tightening cycle may not be over. CPI inflation was 2.9% y/y in Sep-
tember, above the central bank’s target of 2%, while high oil prices,
rapid growth in Seoul housing prices and wage pressures (though
concentrated in the electronics sector) raise the risk of lasting infla -
tion pressure. As economic activity remains robust, supported by strong

exports and recovering investment, the BoK has scope to raise rates
further.
EMEA
Hungary
Monetary status quo and 2027 budget in consolidation mode. The
Central Bank kept its policy rate unchanged at 5.5%, as expected. A cau-
tious stance will likely prevail in the short term. Inflation has been
picking up in recent months, albeit mildly, and will continue to move
upward as the government recently removed the margin cap on fuel.
Separately, the Budget for 2027 and the medium-term fiscal plan show

Hungary’s goal to join the Eurozone; the government expects to reduce
the fiscal deficit to 4.9% of GDP in 2027 (from 7% expected in 2026) and
towards 2.8% of GDP by 2030.
Türkiye
Cautious monetary loosening. The Central Bank lowered its policy rate
by 100 bp to 36% at its October Monetary Policy Committee meeting,
in line with market expectations. The overnight lending and borrowing
rates were also reduced by 100 bp, to 39% and 34.5%, respectively.

The improvement in underlying inflation has yet to translate into a
sustained period of low inflation, justifying the CBRT’s preference for a
measured pace of monetary easing.
LATIN AMERICA
Brazil
President Lula da Silva was re-elected on Sunday for a fourth term
(with 51.8% of the vote), defeating far right-wing candidate Flavio Bol-
sonaro (48.2%). Lula will enter this new term facing significant econo -
mic and political challenges. Public debt has risen to more than 82% of

GDP , while high borrowing costs and rigid mandatory spending leave
limited room to reconcile fiscal consolidation with social spending and
investment. Restoring confidence in the fiscal outlook will be a key
challenge in the short term.
COMMODITIES
Pressure rising. Amid growing rumours of imminent re-escalation of
military operations in the Gulf, energy prices rose sharply this week,
with brent touching $117/b – near its April peak, and European gas

prices (TTF) rising to 97 €/MWh on Monday, their highest level since
Dec 2022. The Asian gas benchmark (JKM) is following the same trend
(4.6% w/w to 31.4 $/mbtu, equivalent to around 96 €/MWh). Diesel
crack spreads are slightly decreasing in Europe (-2.8% w/w on Monday)
and in the US (-4% w/w on Monday). The first Russian diesel tanker
intended to the US market left Russia this Friday and should supply the
US market in 2 weeks. US diesel average retail prices remain oriented

to the upside (0.7% w/w at 6.32 $/gallon on Monday). US commercial
crude inventories fell by 3.1 mb in the week ending 16 October, signi -
ficantly exceeding market expectations for a 340,000-barrel decline.

Editorial 
Increasing signs of a bottom 
Strategy summary: Democrats’ thumping win in the US mid -
term elections has three key implications: i) policy gridlock is 
back in Washington, likely stalling fiscal expansion and new 
tariffs; ii) the chance of a prolonged Middle East conflict is 
curtailed, lowering oil prices and inflation expectations; and  iii) 
the pace of AI data-centre buildout is at risk , curbing AI-linked 
bond supply. Higher corporate and wealth taxes are a long-term 
risk if Democrats win the 2028 presidential elections.   
For portfolios, the election reinforce s our mid-cycle playbook 
already in place:  remaining broadly balanced across assets, 
while looking for tactical opportunities. As the S&P500 volatility 
index (VIX) surpasses 25, we see rising signs of equity market 
bottoming. We add an opportunistic idea on the US IT sector 
(see page 4), while using elevated volatility to earn income 
through structured strategies. In bonds, we prefer the 3-7-year 
part of the Developed Market bond yield curve and BBB-to-BB-
rated corporate bonds to earn attractive income as AI supply 
eases. In FX, we position for a modestly weaker USD over 6 -
12 months by allocating to ex-US assets.  
Policy implications:  Oil fell 12% over the past week as 
Democrats captured the US Senate and House. The party also 
flipped governorships in five states – Georgia, Iowa, Nevada, 
Ohio and Texas – securing a majority of governorships among 
50 US states for the first time since 2010. Vice President Vance 
and Republican leaders attributed the defeat to the Iran conflict, 
making it harder for President Trump to sustain it. Democrat 
control of the defence budget from next year further constrains 
conflict escalation. Despite near-term escalation risks, we see 
the geopolitical risk premium in oil prices fading, with oil easing 
towards USD 80/bbl  over 6-12 months. Oil-driven disinflation 
should allow central banks to adopt less aggressive policies. 
Adding tactical exposure to US IT sector : Semiconductors 
slumped this week amid concerns over data-centre moratoria, 
while beneficiaries of Democrat policies include defensives, 
affordability-related stocks, tariff rollback beneficiaries and  the 
healthcare sector. We use the volatility to add tactical exposure 
to the US IT sector . As we enter mid -cycle dynamics, equities 
should return 8-10% annually. The 2015–2017 mid-cycle offers 
a useful comparison: with an opposition Congress,  S&P 500 
returns slowed to 8.3% , but remained positive. Diversification 
across sectors and regions remains a priority amid the rotation. 
Prefer belly of the bond curve: Government bonds rallied on 
expectations of slower growth. We still expect two more Fed 
hikes by H1 2027, given sticky near-term inflation and a robust 
economy. However, cheaper oil should lower inflation towards 
the Fed’s 2% target next year, allowing rate cuts in H2 2027. 
Ultra-long yields remain elevated due to  fiscal challenges. 
Bonds, particularly those from non-hyperscaler investment -
grade corporates , offer attractive income. The 3 -7-year 
segment – the belly of the yield curve – offers the best balance 
of income and sensitivity to interest-rate changes. 
Credit to benefit from restrained AI supply:  Since states  
control data centre regulations, Democrat wins in governorship 
races in leading data centre states, such as Texas and Georgia, 
means growth in this segment is likely to be restricted. Slower 
data-centre construction could improve credit supply -demand 
conditions due to reduced issuance by hyperscalers. However, 
some data -centre-focused deals could become less viable, 
warranting caution towards lower -quality issuers. High -yield 
credit spreads have widened, but BB- and B-rated bonds have 
held up, while CCC -rated debt has r eached distressed levels. 
We prefer the BBB–BB range, spanning the boundary between 
investment-grade and high-yield debt, for income. 
A modest headwind for the USD, tailwind for gold: The 
dollar should remain range-bound near term before weakening 
modestly over 6-12 months. Democrat control of the Congress 
limits tariff escalation , fiscal expansion and rapid AI buildout , 
three pillars of US exceptionalism supporting the USD through 
2026. Disinflation should also encourage a less aggressive Fed 
stance, removing another source of USD support. Near -term 
support for the USD index (DXY) remains at 100.7 . A weaker-
than-expected payrolls report tonight could accelerate the 
USD’s decline, and vice versa . A softer dollar and sustained 
central-bank buying, led by China, remain supportive of gold.  
—  Rajat Bhattacharya

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 6 November 2026

Top client questions 
  What are the equity market implications of the US mid -
term election results?   
Our view: The US mid -term election outcome adds political 
headwinds but does not alter our outlook for positive equity 
returns. Investors should maintain a Core allocation to global 
equities and continue to diversify their equity exposure across 
regions and sectors. We initiate an opportunistic idea on the US 
information technology (IT) sector, moving our opportunistic 
idea positioning from a de fensive tilt to a more balanced 
positioning. Higher volatility also presents opportunities to use 
structured strategies for equity exposure.  
Rationale: The Democrats’ victory in both chambers of Congress 
has supported the outperformance of themes aligned with their 
policy priorities , with clean energy stocks , including solar 
companies, among the notable beneficiaries. Affordability-related 
themes have also benefited, including companies that could gain 
from potential tariff rollbacks. Medicaid-related stocks  have also 
risen on hopes that healthcare will gain from policy changes. 
Meanwhile, semiconductor stocks remain under pressure  amid 
concerns about potential moratoriums on data centres. Our AI capital 
expenditure (capex) estimates remain conservative relative to 
consensus, but with a potential reset in expectations around data 
centre spending, investors should remain selective.  
We initiate an opportunistic idea on the US IT sector after a 
significant 17% correction this quarter. Historically, this has been 
followed by strong positive returns over the next few quarters ( see 
table). Semiconductors, about 40% of US IT, appears oversold after 
correcting more than 30% since we closed our opportunistic idea a 
month ago. Earnings growth for US IT continues to be solid, with 
volume driven growth in semiconductors, AI monetisation 
opportunities, ongoing cloud growth and resilient services revenue.  
As we highlighted in our November 2026 Global Market Outlook , 
the transition to mid -cycle is typically characterised by greater 
political headwinds. The mid -term election outcome reinforces this 
trend. The mid-cycle experience of 2015-17 offers a useful historical 
parallel for returns that moderate, but remain positive. Although we 
trimmed global equities to a Core allocation last week, we continue 
to expect equities to deliver mid-to-high single-digit returns per 
annum during the mid -cycle, even amid political headwinds, 
supported by steady earnings growth in both 2027 and 2028. 
Stay invested and diversify, with Overweight allocations to the US 
and A xJ, which offer the strongest earnings growth. Sector 
diversification is key as we expect market leadership to broaden. We 
are Overweight the US IT, US healthcare, US financial, US materials, 
Europe ex-UK financial and Japan financial sectors. 
—  Fook Hien Yap, Senior Investment Strategist

Policy uncertainty and a change in the innovation 
cycle is consistent with our view of a mid-cycle 
transition, where equity returns moderate but 
remain positive

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What are the key takeaways from Q3 2026 US earnings? 
Our view:  Q3 US earnings remain robust, but more modest 
earnings beats reinforce our view that equity markets are 
entering a mid-cycle phase. We remain Overweight US equities, 
but recommend investors to maintain diversified sector 
exposure as returns moderate and market leadership broadens. 
Rationale: As of 30 October, 82% of S&P500 companies that ha d 
reported had beaten earnings estimates. However, aggregate 
earnings were just 4.5% above expectations, a smaller beat than in 
recent quarters. Still, Q3 US earnings growth remain s robust at 
31.6% y/y, supporting expected full-year growth of 36.1% in 2026 
and 16.4% in 2027 for the S&P500. The slower pace of upgrades 
suggests the bull market is maturing  as investors focus on the 
sustainability of earnings growth. This raises the hurdle for positive 
share price reactions and makes selectivity more essential. For Big 
Tech, capex commitments remain high, while free cash flow is under 
pressure and expected to remain negative. Financing risks weigh on 
sentiment, particularly for companies with weaker balance sheets.  
We remain Overweight US equities , given the strong earnings 
growth, but we expect elevated volatility  and modest returns. We 
prefer quality exposure within technology  while maintaining  
diversified exposure across US financials, healthcare and materials. 
—  Ryan Goh, Investment Strategist 
  What to expect from Japanese banks’ upcoming results?   
Our view:  We expect  Japanese banks’  earnings growth  to 
continue over the medium term, supported by higher domestic 
net interest income (NII) and resilient fee income. We remain 
positive on the sector, favouring banks with strong deposit 
franchises and scope to increase shareholder returns. 
Investors will focus on whether full -year guidance upgrades 
and buybacks exceed expectations. 
Rationale: The BoJ’s September rate hike to 1.25% took effect late 
in the quarter, so its benefit should be more visible from the 
December quarter as loans reprice. We expect three further BoJ 
rate hikes by June 2027, supporting further NII expansion.  We 
will also look for signs that stronger corporate investment is driving 
faster loan growth and higher financing and advisory fees.  
We prefer to place more weight on guidance upgrades driven by 
recurring lending and fee income than on those driven by gains on 
equity disposals. Continued reductions in cross -shareholdings 
could free up capital for additional buybacks, providing a further 
boost to return on equity  (ROE). Key risks:  faster-than-expected 
deposit rate repricing, rising credit costs and bond portfolio losses. 
—  Robin Xie, CFA, Senior Investment Strategist

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 6 November 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  What are the fundamental reasons behind your India 
equity upgrade? Which sectors offer favourable risk-reward? 
Our View: Indian equities offer an increasingly attractive risk -
reward profile. With India’s valuation premium to Emerging 
Markets (EMs) narrowing, foreign ownership near decade lows, 
Underweight EM positioning  and a stabilising Indian rupee 
(INR), conditions are increasingly supportive of Indian equity 
outperformance vs. the broader AxJ region.  
Rationale: We have upgraded Indian equities to Overweight within 
our AxJ equities allocation. While Indian markets  have witnessed 
USD 32bn of foreign investor outflows in 2026 year-to-date, strong 
domestic institutional investor inflows of USD 74bn  have more 
than offset foreign selling. These outflows and the Indian equity 
market’s underperformance vs . global peers reflect broader EM 
allocation shifts towards AI , rather than weakening India n 
fundamentals. Capital has gravitated toward s markets such as the 
US, Taiwan and  South Korea, which offer direct exposure to AI 
beneficiaries. However, we expect market leadership to broaden, as 
the rapid AI buildout is increasing ly debt-funded, and US mid-term 
election results raise uncertainty. We prefer to diversify and we see 
investors reallocating towards structurally strong  growth markets 
such as India, which offer non-AI driven growth.  
The RBI’s measures have supported INR stability,  but underlying 
USD demand remains elevated. Lower oil prices would improve 
external balances, but the INR is likely to follow a path of gradual, 
controlled depreciation rather than a quick downward move. On 
valuation to its own historical average, the Nifty 50 one-year forward 
P/E is trading 18% below its long-term average while ROE at 14.7% 
is above historical averages. With India now marginally Underweight 
in large EM portfolios compared to  the c.2% average Overweight 
historically, the case for a recovery in foreign flows is 
strengthening. 
India’s earnings growth remains intact . The Nifty 50 clocked Q1 
FY27 and Q2 FY27 profit-after-tax growth of c.20% y/y (ex -oil 
marketing companies) . Large -caps offer greater earnings visibility 
and downside protection, while stronger growth in small - and mid-
caps warrants a selective approach given elevated valuations.  
India’s growth outlook remains supported by a strengthening 
investment cycle, manufacturing momentum, supply chain 
diversification, resilient consumption and healthy corporate and 
banking balance sheets. Importantly, earnings growth remains 
robust and broa d-based across the financial, metals, telecom, 
automobiles and other domestic sectors, with Nifty 50 earnings 
growth expectations of c.14-15% over 2027 vis a vis A xJ’s 
technology driven earnings growth of 29% in 2027. 
—  Shaily Gang, Chief Investment Strategist, India

### Page 7

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 6 November 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Does the emerging US job market data alter your view of 
the Fed’s rate path and government bond yields? 
Our view: We do not expect the recent and incoming US job 
market data to alter our outlook unless there are significant 
deviations from the US economy’s current trajectory.  
Rationale: The US economy continues to exhibit resilience, with the 
latest Job Openings and Labour Turnover Survey data showing job 
openings surprising to the upside at 7.5mn. Initial jobless claims 
remain near three-month lows. There is little evidence of a material 
slowdown in labour demand. Inflation is expected to trend lower 
through H2 2027, but the disinflationary impulse remains a medium-
term story and is unlikely to manifest meaningfully in the near term. 
The market is now pricing in two additional 25bps hikes by H1 
2027 (down from three previously), in line with our view. These two 
rate hikes are largely priced into the US government bond yield 
curve. We estimate 2- and 10-year US government bond yields to 
range between 4.5-4.75% and 5-5.25%, respectively, over the next 
three months . Our twelve -month forecast for 2- and 10 -year US 
government bond yields are 4.25-4.5% and 4.75-5%, respectively, 
The 2-year yield is already trading within our expected range and 
would only move materially higher if a significantly stronger -than-
expected payrolls print led markets to price in a third hike. With two 
hikes baked in, long -run disinflation expectations intact and an 
uncertain fiscal outlook, the longer end of the  yield curve is 
expected to be less sensitive to a single payrolls release. 
—  Ray Heung, Senior Investment Strategist 
  What is your outlook for the GBP following the BoE’s rate 
hike this week? Do you see any impact on EUR/GBP? 
Our view: The BoE  rate hike supports  the GBP, but upside 
remains limited. We remain constructive on EUR/GBP, 
expecting a rebound towards 0.8450. 
Rationale: The BoE hiked by 25bps to 4% as expected . The GBP 
was little changed, despite hawkish inflation guidance. With markets 
pricing only around 50bps of additional BoE tightening over the next 
12 months, we see the GBP remaining vulnerable. In contrast, the 
ECB held its deposit rate at 2.50% in October but signalled readiness 
to tighten if inflation remains elevated. We expect Euro area inflation 
to rise further, keeping the ECB’s tightening bias alive. This suggests 
EUR/GBP has fallen too far relative to interest rate differentials.  
Germany’s planned increase in federal debt issuance  should 
support Euro area growth, while Moody’s decision not to downgrade 
France has removed an important EUR tail risk. Meanwhile, tighter 
UK fiscal policy is likely to weigh on domestic growth and business 
investment. We expect EUR/GBP to recover towards 0.8450. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

We expect 2- and 10-year US government bond 
yields to range between 4.5-4.75% and 5-5.25%, 
respectively, over the next three months

### Page 8

Standard Chartered Bank 
WS Global CIO Office | 6 November 2026

Top client questions (cont’d) 
  Why is the AUD underperforming despite the RBA hike? 
Our view: The AUD has lagged because markets had largely 
priced in the RBA’s November rate hike to 4.85%, and the bank 
gave investors less reason to expect another. Equity weakness 
also weighed on the currency. We remain bullish on AUD/USD  
and would add on pullbacks. Australia’s interest rate advantage 
and a sustained recovery in risk sentiment should support a 
move towards 0.72 in three months and 0.74 in 12 months. 
Rationale: Ahead of the 3 November RBA rate decision, market 
pricing implied an 88% chance of a 25bp s hike, leaving little scope 
for an upside surprise. What changed after the announcement 
was the pricing of the next hike.  The RBA dropped its reference 
to further rate increases, and three members preferred a rate hold. 
Markets scaled back the odds of rates reaching 5.10% next year to 
around 30%, thus weighing on the AUD. 
The AUD is sensitive to swings in global risk appetite, especially 
the US tech sell-off spread to global equities, triggering margin calls 
and weighing on  the currency. An equity recovery would ease that 
pressure. Even after the two 25bp s Fed hikes we expect by mid -
2027, Australia would retain a 35bps rate advantage  over the top 
of the Fed’s target range, supporting a gradual AUD recovery. 
—  Vincent Tan, Senior Investment Strategist 
—  Iris Yuen, Investment Strategist

We see AUD/USD upside risks on a sustained 
recovery in risk sentiment

The Week Ahead October 23, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         2

CENTRAL BANK MEETINGS

Bank of Thailand (BOT) (October 28)

We expect the BOT to maintain its policy 
rate at 1.00% at its 28 October meeting, 
extending its pause for a fourth 
consecutive decision as it balances 
inflationary pressures against uneven 
and subdued economic growth. Our view 
is aligned with fixed market income 
pricing. Headline inflation rose anew to 
2.8% yoy in September 2026, driven by 
higher energy, food, and core price 
increases, but remained within the upper 
end of the central bank’s 1 -3% target 
range. As the BOT is still likely to look 
through what it views as temporary, 
supply-driven inflationary pressu res, it 
will likely refrain from tightening policy, 
and keep interest rates accommodative 
to support the soft and uneven growth 
cycle.

European Central Bank (October 29)

We expect the ECB to extend its pause, 
keeping the deposit facility rate at 2.5%. 
Market participants will instead focus on 
the central bank’s assessment of rising 
French and Italian bond yields and 
widening spreads versus Bunds. While 
we do not expect dir ect references to 
individual countries, the ECB is likely to 
signal that recent developments in 
France reflect the country’s weaker 
fiscal and debt profile rather than a 
broader systemic concern. Policymakers 
will seek to differentiate between a 
localised bond sell -off and a Eurozone -
wide market disruption, justifying their 
decision to monitor developments rather 
than implement specific measures at this 
juncture. Meanwhile, inflation continues 
to rise, with little relief expected in 
upcoming readings given elevated 
global fuel prices. 
Bank of Japan (October 30)

The Bank of Japan is expected to keep 
the overnight call rate unchanged at 1.25% 
at this meeting, pausing after the 25bp 
hike in September. The BOJ is likely to 
assess the impact of the previous rate 
hike before taking further action. The 
recent stabilisat ion of the yen also 
reduces the urgency of another hike at 
this meeting.

The tightening bias should remain intact. 
Growth and inflation remain broadly on 
track, leaving room for the BOJ to revise 
up its FY2026 –FY2027 GDP and CPI 
forecasts. Wage growth has also 
remained strong, with Rengo reportedly 
targeting wage increases of 6% in 2027, 
reinforcing the BOJ’s confidence that 
underlying inflation can be sustained 
around its 2% target. We continue to 
expect the policy rate to rise to 1.50% in 
December 2026 or January 2027, 
followed by a further increase to 1.75% 
by 2Q 2027.

FORTHCOMING DATA 
RELEASES

Hong Kong SAR

Export growth is expected to accelerate 
from 53.0% yoy in August to 55.0% in 
September, in line with the pickup in 
China’s export growth from 25.0% to 
36.3%. As a key re-export hub for China,

### Page 3

The Week Ahead October 23, 2026 
                                                                                                                                         3

Hong Kong should benefit from robust 
AI-related electronics trade, with 
electrical machinery, equipment and 
parts exports to Hong Kong accounting 
for 20.6% of China’s total exports. 
Import growth is also expected to remain 
firm, supported by resilient expo rt 
orders and gradually improving 
investment sentiment, sustaining 
demand for intermediate and capital 
goods.

Singapore

Singapore’s industrial production (IP) 
likely remained in expansion for a 13th 
consecutive month in September 2026, 
although we expect growth to moderate 
to 7.6% yoy from August’s strong 15.4% 
yoy pace. IP growth continued to be 
underpinned by the electronics and 
precision engineering clusters, which 
has been key drivers of Singapore’s 
manufacturing strength, reflecting 
sustained demand for artificial 
intelligence-related hardware. However, 
performance across clusters remained 
uneven, with chemicals and biomedical 
manufacturing likely contracting, and 
tempering overall gains.

South Korea

The preliminary estimate for 3Q26 GDP 
is likely to show growth remaining solid 
at 3.2% yoy , slightly below 3.7% in 2Q 
and 3.8% in 1Q. Nominal GDP growth is 
likely to remain in double digits, 
significantly outpacing real GDP growth, 
thanks to improved terms of trade amid 
rising memory export prices. By 
expenditure, exports picked up in 3Q in 
both nominal and real terms, supported 
by stronger semiconductor shipments. 
Investment also appeared robust, as 
reflected in the equipment investment 
estimation index and machinery order 
index. Private consumption, however, 
appeared to weaken amid the sharp 
KOSPI correction since July following  
the introduction of leveraged ETF 
regulations. Our full -year GDP growth 
forecasts of 3.5% for 2026 and 2.4% for 
2027 remain on track.

Taiwan

The preliminary estimate for 3Q GDP is 
likely to show growth remaining strong 
at 12 –13% yoy , close to 12.9% in 2Q, 
albeit below the peak of 15.4% in 1Q. 
Exports maintained strong double -digit 
growth in 3Q (44.6% yoy  in nominal 
terms and 21.1% in real terms), supported 
by robust shipments of semiconductors, 
servers and other AI -related products. 
Machinery investment also sustained 
strong double-digit growth, underpinned 
by higher capacity utilisation in the ICT 
sector and a resulting increase in capital 
goods imports. Private consumption 
appeared to have strengthened further, 
supported by stock market gains, 
improving wage growth, and the 
associated wealth and income effects. 
Construction investment remained the 
weak s pot, weighed down by elevated 
inventories in the new-home market and 
the ongoing need for inventory digestion. 
We maintain our 2026 and 2027 GDP 
growth forecasts at 11.6% and 5.6%, 
respectively, with risks tilted to the 
upside.

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026EDITORIAL
US MIDTERMS: THE COST OF LIVING IS NOW A PIVOTAL ISSUE
1 AP-NORC, October 2026.
2 Jared Bernstein, Daniel Posthumus, ‘The way we were: Price-level shocks and consumers’ memories’, SIEPR, May 2026.
3 72% of registered voters rank ending the war with Iran as one of the top priorities for the next Congress, including 60% of Republicans (AP-NORC, October 2026).

4 Oleg Korenok, David Munro, Jiayi Chen, ‘Inflation and attention thresholds’, GLO Discussion Paper Series, No. 1175, Global Labour Organization, 2022.
5 Rajashri Chakrabarti, Thu Pham, Beck Pierce, Maxim L. Pinkovskiy, ‘Tracking the K-shaped economy: who’s driving spending?’, Federal Reserve Bank of New York, Liberty
Street Economics, 1st May 2026.
6 The year 2020 is excluded due to the disruptions caused by lockdowns.
In the United States, growth is holding up, unemployment remains low and consumer spending shows no sign of slowing.

On paper, that should flatter the president’s party ahead of the midterms. Yet, Republicans could lose control of Congress.
The rise in the cost of living, which weighs on households far more than macroeconomic aggregates suggest, could be one
of the reasons. On top of persistent inflation, rekindled by the war in Iran, there is also a slowdown in wage growth and
rising housing costs. The election could hinge on affordability, yet whichever party wins will lack the means to address it.

A change in the majority in Congress will nonetheless have an impact on the country’s economy.
On 3 rd November, Americans will elect a new Congress for the next
two years. With Republicans currently in control of the White House,
the House of Representatives and the Senate, the balance of power in
Washington could shift. Polls suggest that the House, at least, could
swing to the Democrats. Beyond the new balance of power, the vote
will reflect how voters view the way the country is being run. Here, we

focus on the cost of living.
INFLATION: THE INCUMBENTS’ BURDEN
The incumbent party goes into the election with two handicaps.
The first is structural: no matter who holds the White House, the pre -
sident’s party almost always loses seats in midterm elections. The
second stems from the combination of robust but uneven growth
and persistent inflation, which is undermining household confidence.
Pre-election polls1 confirm this assessment: the economy and the cost-

of-living top voters’ worries.
Inflation has overshot the Fed’s target every year since 2021. Prices are
up 28% over five years: 31% for housing (not including mortgage rates,
which have more than doubled), and 29% for food. This shock is felt all
the more keenly as it is the most severe in forty years and follows on
from decades of low inflation2. Furthermore, it hits the lowest-income
households the hardest. In addition, the election comes after an ener-

gy-driven rise in inflation. Energy accounts for the entire gap between
the latest CPI reading (3.7% y/y in September) and the last figure re -
corded before the conflict in the Middle East started (2.4% y/y).
Fuel has made things worse. The rise in the price of a gallon (46%
between February and October) caused by the Iran war — which is
unpopular even amongst Republican voters3 — is an aggravating factor.
In 2026, inflation only slightly crossed the ‘attention threshold’, the

3.6%y/y rate beyond which households start to regard it as problema -
tic4. Fuel is a recurring, unavoidable expense whose price is directly
observable, unlike many items in the CPI basket. This results in a subs-
tantial gap between perceived inflation and measured inflation.
Inflation must be viewed alongside the other drivers of households’
well-being. Today, wages, payrolls and the housing market are moving
the wrong way for most Americans.
IT'S NOT JUST PRICES

The 2022 midterms suggest caution in reading the polls. Democrats
defied expectations of a ‘Republican wave’, retaining control of the Se-
nate and losing the House by just a few seats, even though inflation
averaged 8% that year. This reminds us that inflation must be viewed
alongside the other drivers of households’ well-being. Indeed, how
people experience the cost of living depends on the interplay between
prices, wages and housing. In 2022, wages, payrolls and the housing

market were all growing faster than in 2026. Today, all three are mo -
ving the wrong way for most Americans.
Wage growth is slowing just as inflation picks up again. In September
2026, average hourly earnings were up 3% y/y in nominal terms, the
slowest rate since 2018, and now below inflation. Real wage earnings
are therefore falling (-0.7% y/y in September, with six out of nine mon-
ths showing a decline in 2026). The rise in the cost of living is thus

compounded by a net loss of purchasing power. The Atlanta Fed reports
that wage growth is lower for the bottom quartile of earners (the 25%
of employees with the lowest pay) than for the top quartile (the 25%
with the highest pay): 3.8% y/y vs. 4.4% on average since late 2024,
following 10 years in which the gap favoured the bottom quartile. Top
earners also benefit most from the wealth effect linked to stock mar -
ket valuations. This explains the resilience of aggregate consumption.

However, this rests on a relatively narrow base, mostly households
earning more than USD 125,000/year5. Meanwhile, a falling saving rate
(3.8% in September 2026, down 1.4pp y/y) suggests households are
drawing on their savings to offset price rises. A large share of the po -
pulation is therefore excluded from the growth that has been observed.
Beyond wages, there is the question of employment prospects.
The unemployment rate remains low and consistent with full employ-

ment. Yet the labour market looks stagnant: low firing despite repeated
shocks and low hiring despite solid growth. Payroll growth (an average
of 68,000 per month since January) is well below that of the previous
decade (nearly 200,000 per month between 2011 and 2019 6). Com-
bined with a workforce that is no longer growing due to an ageing
population and a restrictive immigration policy, this is enough to keep
the unemployment rate low and the labour market looking healthy.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.40 / 2 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
But households are likely to be suffering from a lack of opportunities,
and consequently from a lack of prospects for wage growth that would
help them to increase their purchasing power. The Conference Board’s
labour differential, a gauge of perceived job availability, says as much:
outside the pandemic, it is at its lowest since 2015.
Housing is the area that has deteriorated the most. The rise in

mortgage rates that began in 2022, from under 4% to over 7%, has
dampened the property market. This is true on the demand side, as
expected, but also on the supply side. The sharp rise in interest rates
is deterring homeowners with low-interest mortgages from selling, as
they would have to borrow again at current rates. This ‘lock-in effect’
prevents prices from falling despite weaker demand. The lock-in effect
is also affecting tenants, as the decline in the availability of housing is

driving up rents. Housing lies at the heart of the disconnect between
the macroeconomic picture and voters’ perception of it, as they face a
multifaceted rise in the cost of living. That rise is also driven by credit:
monthly repayments on a home of the same value have risen shar -
ply. This additional cost, which inflation figures do not capture7, erodes
households’ purchasing power in the property market.
A CONGRESS POWERLESS ON THE COST OF LIVING

The next Congress will, in all likelihood, have little room for ma -
noeuvre. Neither party is within reach of the thresholds required to
override the filibuster (which allows 41 out of 100 senators to block
a bill from coming to a vote) and the presidential veto (two-thirds of
both chambers).
Affordability is likely to be a key issue in the election, but the winners
will lack the tools to address it. The Democrats’ agenda relies notably
on the reintroduction of schemes that were abolished or scaled back

under the current administration (Affordable Care Act tax credits, Me-
dicaid). Even if Congress passed them, they would be subject to a pre-
sidential veto. There is no question of direct support for households or
a restrictive fiscal policy.
Whether inflation falls will depend on two factors: how the conflict in
Iran unfolds and the effectiveness of monetary policy. The labour mar-
ket should hold its current balance: robust growth (see our EcoFlash)

is supporting demand, whilst failing to alleviate supply-side pressures,
and the unemployment rate could continue to fall. Household senti -
ment will continue to depend on how prices develop. As for housing,
it is unlikely to improve in the short term. With the 10-year Treasury
yield still at 4.8% at the end of 2027 (BNP Paribas forecast), 30-year
mortgage rates would, at best, remain around 7%.
THE OTHER ECONOMIC STAKES OF THE VOTE
Historically, divided governments have brought legislative stability,

which is viewed favourably by the markets. But polarisation has in -
creased in recent years and is likely to feature prominently in the bud-
get vote and the debt-ceiling debate. The debt limit, in particular, could
be reached as early as Q1 2027, depending on the Treasury’s borrowing
requirements. The current administration, meanwhile, has taken major
decisions (on tariffs, immigration and Iran) without involving Congress,
a practice that is likely to continue.

7 Marijn A. Bolhuis, Judd N. L. Cramer, Karl Oskar Schulz, Lawrence H. Summers, ‘The cost of money is part of the cost of living: new evidence on the consumer sentiment
anomaly’, NBER Working Paper, No. 32163, 2024.
Risks to the deficit remain tilted to the upside. According to our base -
line scenario, it is expected to remain stable at 6.3% of GDP in 2027,
but the post-election balance of power could add to the cost of the war
in Iran and rising debt-service costs. In particular, interests could align

in favour of increased spending should the Democrats gain control of
both houses of Congress. Avoiding, or ending, a shutdown could require
a trade-off involving mutual concessions from the presidency on key,
popular aspects of the Democratic agenda (such as healthcare), and
from the Democrats on defence.
The AI sector is exposed to the vote. The economic stakes are high
given how much the associated investment cycle and the wealth effect
contribute to US growth. However, at this stage, the risk is not legisla-

tive. As with the cost of living, sweeping legislation is unlikely, all the
more so with a president holding a pro-AI stance, and who holds the
right of veto. The risk lies rather in sentiment and further out. A Demo-
cratic sweep could lead to increased congressional activism on regu -
lation, on concentration in the AI industry, and on the knock-on effects
on the cost of living (data centres and electricity) and on jobs. Putting
these issues on the agenda could create a climate conducive to tighter

regulation in the medium term and weigh on the sector’s valuations.
Furthermore, the mid-term elections will see 36 governors’ seats up
for election. These governors have extensive powers (regarding autho-
risations and incentives) when it comes to the establishment of data
centres in their states.
Finally, the midterms will matter for monetary policy. The Senate
confirms, by simple majority, the president’s nominees to the Fed’s
Board of Governors. Consequently, should the Senate come under De -

mocratic control, President Trump would need a consensus pick to re-
place Governor Powell from 2028 — or to fill any other seat that might
become vacant during the next Congress.
*****
Whatever the outcome of the election, disinflation and, more broadly,
relief from the cost of living, depend less on Congress than on the
White House and, to a lesser extent, the Fed. The key factor lies in the
resolution of the conflict in Iran, coupled with an effective monetary

policy to enable a sustainable return to price stability.
Anis Bensaidani
anis.bensaidani@bnpparibas.com

Document 470: Why China resists rebalancing its growth model_ (2026-10-26)

Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-26
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026EDITORIAL
WHY CHINA RESISTS REBALANCING ITS GROWTH MODEL?
China has never exported as much as it does now, and its trade surplus has never been higher. At the same time, its
economic growth is slowing, held back by weak domestic demand. The imbalances in China’s growth model, and the ex-
ternal imbalances they give rise to, are therefore continuing to worsen. With Europe taking a tougher line and the United

States, with which China shares nothing more than ‘strategic stability’, the standoff looks set to continue. However, the
European Union and China would have a great deal to lose from a lasting deterioration in their relationship. It is in the
interests of both parties to seek to restore the balance. To this end, it is important to understand Beijing’s objectives and
the constraints facing China. Why does the country resist adjusting its growth model? Whilst exports are a vital asset for

sustaining economic activity and asserting China’s technological and industrial power, this strategy also comes at a cost.
IMBALANCES ARE WORSENING
A K-shaped economy. China’s economic growth slowed to 4.6% year-
on-year (y/y) over the first three quarters of 2026, from 5.0% in 2025.
More worryingly, the gap is widening between weak domestic demand
and the very strong performance of exports, driven by artificial intelli-
gence equipment and green technologies. Furthermore, the dynamism

of export-oriented industries has not, so far, had any positive knock-on
effects on other sectors or on households.
Domestic demand is struggling. The property crisis, now in its sixth
year, and a sluggish labour market are weighing on household confi -
dence and consumption. Since the start of 2026, households have been
prioritising debt reduction (bank loans to households have fallen by
1% y/y), house sales have continued to fall, and retail sales have been

stagnating (Chart 1). On the investment front, the decline is becoming
more pronounced and widespread: it is affecting not only the property
sector but also infrastructure and many manufacturing sectors (with
the exception of the textile industry, transport and communications,
and electronic and electrical equipment).
Exports remain very buoyant. Exports continue to be the main driver of
growth (their net contribution is expected to account for around a third

of real GDP growth in 2026, as it did in 2024–2025). Against a backdrop
of rebound in global trade, Chinese companies maintained their market
share at 14.6% of total exports (measured in US dollars) in the first
half of 2026, as they had in 2025. Growth in goods exports accelerated
(21.9% y/y in USD over the first nine months, compared with 5.2% in
2025), driven by both volumes and prices. These have rebounded after
three years of decline, driven by rising costs of raw materials and the

prices of goods required for AI, but they have increased at a slower rate
than the global price average. Exports from high-tech sectors (batte -
ries, electric vehicles, robots and AI equipment) are posting the highest
growth rates (+55.8% y/y for all AI-related goods over the first nine
months of 2026).
Record external surpluses. Imports, which had been stagnating since
2022, have been recovering since the start of the year (up 27.2% y/y
over the first nine months), also driven by both volumes and prices.

The countries benefiting from this rebound in the Chinese market are
commodity exporters and Asian producers integrated into AI value
chains (South Korea in particular). Otherwise, China has significantly
reduced its reliance on imports over the past few years. Overall, the
trade surplus has grown at a slower rate in 2026 than in previous years.
Nevertheless, it has still reached a record high: USD1,249 billion over
the twelve months to September 2026, which is three times its 2019

level (see Chart 2).

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.39 / 26 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
Meanwhile, the current account surplus is expected to approach a re -
cord level of USD 800 billion in 2026, representing nearly 4% of China’s
GDP (a ratio already seen in the late 2000s). As a proportion of global
GDP , this is unprecedented (at 0.6%).
Imbalances set to persist. The imbalances inherent in the growth mo-
del are likely to persist, fuelled notably by the strong price and non-

price competitiveness of Chinese goods. This competitiveness remains
underpinned by: i/ innovation efforts and the move up the value chain
in high-tech sectors and the low-carbon transition; ii/ low production
costs (labour, low-cost credit, land and energy, tax benefits 1); iii/ the
yuan’s exchange rate. Its slow appreciation since mid-2025 has not
offset the cumulative depreciation from 2022 to 2025 (nominal and
real depreciation due to low inflation), and its real effective exchange

rate (REER) remains 10% below its 2021 average. At the end of 2025,
the IMF estimated that the REER was undervalued by between 12%
and 20%.
Beijing’s economic strategy is exacerbating imbalances. The 15th Five-
Year Plan2, launched this year, prioritises innovation, high-tech (ex -
port-oriented) sectors and technological self-reliance above all else,
whilst reforms to the social security system and private consumption
remain on the back burner (see Chart 3). Furthermore, fiscal policy,

which had provided moderate support for household spending for a
time in 2025, was tightened in 2026 (until the recent announcements
of new measures for the final quarter).
WHY BEIJING IS DIGGING ITS HEELS IN
Beijing is not giving ground on any key issues. Faced with trading
partners calling on China to rebalance its economic model, reduce in -
dustrial subsidies and strengthen the yuan, Beijing responds that the
trade surplus reflects the competitiveness of its goods, points to the

United States’ insufficient savings, and uses critical materials as a bar-
gaining chip in negotiations.
The need for growth. Exports are a key driver of growth at a time when
the Chinese economy is undergoing a period of transition. The country
has moved away from its pre-2022 model, which was based on in -
vestment in property and traditional industries and financed by debt.
Growth is now driven by ‘new productive forces’, and exports must pro-

vide producers with the market that domestic demand does not. Fur -
thermore, innovation and the roll-out of AI must stimulate productivity
gains and offset the impact of the shrinking labour force on economic
growth. It is essential to limit the slowdown of growth in order to sup-
port job creation and absorb the economy’s excess debt.
The strategic argument. Exports are a means of asserting China’s in -
dustrial and technological supremacy, weakening its competitors, and

strengthening ‘national security’. They place China at the heart of va -
lue chains in the most strategic industries, such as AI and the low-car-
bon transition. Furthermore, the pursuit of self-sufficiency and import
substitution — which exacerbate external imbalances — also help to
build national sovereignty by reducing dependencies.
A political and fiscal choice. Expanding the social safety net is seen as
a solution for providing long-term support for household consumption3

by reducing their savings rate (estimated at 35.7% of disposable income
in 2025, which is more than double the average rate for the euro area).
1 According to the IMF, the cost of China’s industrial policy amounts to about 4% of GDP . See IMF, ‘Industrial Policy in China: Quantification and Impact on Misalloca-
tion’, August 2025: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229017855.001
2 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, China’s 15th Five-Year Plan: prioritising power over addressing imbalances

3 The IMF estimates, for example, that doubling social expenditure (health, education, social services and basic pensions) in rural areas could reduce savings and
raise the ratio of private consumption to GDP by 1.8 pp in the medium term, at an annual fiscal cost of 1% of GDP . See IMF, People’s Republic of China 2025 Article IV,
February 2026: https://doi.org/10.5089/9798229038911.002
However, this is not a priority for Beijing, certainly due to ‘ideologi -

cal’ mistrust of the welfare state and a desire for fiscal prudence.
A major reform of the healthcare or pension systems would place a
strain on public finances in both the short and long term, particularly
as the population ages. Whilst the central government remains in a
sound financial position, local governments are already under strain
following several years of falling land sales proceeds, high deficits and
rising debt. The authorities do not have a large fiscal room for ma -

noeuvre and wish to preserve it in anticipation of future shocks.
LIMITATIONS AND POSSIBLE ADJUSTMENTS
In China, a society gripped by anxiety… Economic imbalances are ta -
king their toll on households. The emphasis placed on manufacturing
rather than services is having a negative impact on employment and
wages (the share of value added allocated to employees’ pay is esti -
mated at 50% in the services sector and 41% in manufacturing). The
downturn in the property market is eroding households’ wealth, and

their savings are mainly held in low-yield bank accounts. Finally, social
spending is rising slowly (by 5.2% a year since 2020, compared with
9.7% over the previous five years).
… and enterprises under pressure. The low prices of Chinese goods,
both for export and on the domestic market — resulting from excess
production capacity and ‘involution’ — are eroding corporate profit
margins. Total profits for industrial enterprises have recovered this
year but they are highly concentrated, particularly in AI-related sec -

tors, while the proportion of industrial companies reporting losses has
continued to rise (30%, compared with 26% in 2022). These vulnerabili-
ties are having increasing contagion effects on the rest of the economy,
notably through worsening payment delays and rising pressure on the
profitability of financial institutions.

for a changing
world
Abroad, trading partners are becoming increasingly intolerant. This
is particularly the case of the United States and the European Union 4,
which have absorbed 24% and 28% respectively of China’s trade surplus
over the last twelve months (data from China’s General Administration
of Customs). Tensions between the US and China could rise again in
2027 when the current truce (which has mainly led to a reduction in

tariffs following the peak in H1 2025) comes to an end. Against a back-
drop of competition for technological supremacy and industrial sove -
reignty, the trend towards decoupling between China and the US could
continue, thereby reducing market opportunities for Chinese producers.
Europe, for its part, is reinforcing its strategic approach and expanding
its toolkit of measures, moving towards more targeted restrictions and
stricter market access conditions, especially in case of serious market

distortions). Rather than pursuing a strategy of decoupling, the EU is
seeking to reduce risks and provide greater protection for critical sec -
tors (including the automotive and chemical industries) without clo -
sing its market and whilst continuing to attract Chinese investment.
This strategy will also lead to tensions. With imbalances becoming
increasingly unsustainable, what course of action would make it pos -
sible to avoid a sudden adjustment precipitated by episodes of crises?

4 See BNP Paribas, EcoWeek, When it comes to China, Europe should focus less on its trade deficits and more on its investments
A ‘concerted’ adjustment is difficult to envisage. A negotiated adjust -
ment, along the lines of the 1985 Plaza agreements, seems unlikely in
the current international and military context. Furthermore, China and
its trading partners largely disagree on the diagnosis and the causes of
the imbalances. Moreover, a simple revaluation of the yuan would, on

its own, have only a limited effect on China’s competitiveness.
A gradual adjustment may be possible… in the long term. However, the
reduction of imbalances will need to come both from partner countries
(through increased savings) and from China. In the short term, China
can ease trade tensions by investing more abroad, which is all the
easier given that it has huge current account surpluses. This would
enable it to maintain its access to foreign markets and support the

production capacity of its trading partners. More structural changes
(a more flexible exchange rate regime, less fiscal support for indus -
tries and more support for households) will only be feasible once Bei -
jing considers that the costs of its export-led model will outweigh the
strategic gains. A key challenge for trading partners will be to work out
how to shift this cost-benefit balance.
Christine Peltier
christine.peltier@bnpparibas.com

Document 476: 「関西の景気動向」９月号 AI関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産（PDF：841KB）

Source PDF: all report pdf/「関西の景気動向」９月号　AI関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産（PDF：841KB）.pdf

2026 年９月

１．景気の現状と展望 
 関西の景気は、緩やかな回復基調を維持しつつも、一部に弱い動きがみられる。企業部門では、円安傾向に
加えて、世界的なAI関連需要の拡大を背景に輸出額は増加している。AI関連投資の活発化などを背景に、
鉱工業生産にも持ち直しの動きがみられる。設備投資は、中東情勢の混乱などによる先行き不透明感が意識
されるなかでも、企業の収益計画が高めの水準を維持するもとで拡大基調が続いている。一方、家計部門では、
所得環境の改善が続くものの物価高が重石となり、個人消費の回復ペースは緩慢にとどまっている。 
先行きを展望すると、中東情勢の混乱が長引くなか、資源価格の高止まりが企業活動の重石となる。一方、
世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大が関西の生産や輸出に波及する動きが続き、設備投資の押し上げにもつながる
と見込まれる。個人消費は、持続的な賃上げの動きが下支えに寄与するものの、物価高による下押し圧力が
根強く、回復ペースは引き続き緩慢にとどまる見通しである。

２．個別指標の動き 
（１） 輸出・生産動向 
関西の輸出額（円ベース）は大きく増加している。国・地域別にみると、中国（香港を含む）向けや、アジ
ア（除く中国・香港）向けの伸びが大きく、ともに半導体等電子部品の増加がけん引している。米国向けは振
れを伴いつつも増加傾向にある。品目別にみると、電気機器では半導体等電子部品、一般機械では半導体
等製造装置の増加が顕著にみられ、世界的な AI 関連需要の高まりが押し上げに寄与しているとみられる。金
属及び同製品も先端材料としての需要増や、それに伴う市況の高騰を背景に高水準で推移している。 
一方、輸出を数量面でみると、回復傾向にあるものの、ペースは緩やかにとどまっている。中国向けやアジア
NIEs 向けは低水準での推移が続いているほか、ASEAN 向けも伸び悩んでいる。米国向けは振れを伴いつつ
も、基調としては底堅く推移している。日本銀行が公表している実質輸出指数にも増勢の加速はみられない。

以上のように、輸出数量の回復が緩慢にとどまる一方、輸出金額が大幅に増加しており、両者の動きに乖離
がみられる。これは、円安による輸出金額の押し上げや、AI 関連需要の拡大を受けた関連財の価格高騰によ
る影響が大きい。

鉱工業生産は、持ち直しの動きが続いている。業種別にみると、半導体製造装置やデータセンター向け設備
など AI 関連需要の裾野の広がりが追い風となっている生産用機械工業や電気・情報通信機械工業、機能性
素材や医薬品の増産を受けた化学工業の上振れが目立つ。一方、電子部品・デバイス工業は依然として低
水準での推移が続いている（関西における電子部品・デバイス生産の低迷について、詳しくは後述のトピックス
を参照）。

（２） 企業収益・設備投資動向 
企業収益を日本銀行大阪支店「短観」（６月調査、全規模・全産業）でみると、関西企業全体の 2026
年度の経常利益（全産業・全規模）は前年度比▲12.9％と、減益見通しとなった。中東情勢の混乱が長
引くなか資源価格の高止まりなどが収益の下押し圧力となっている。もっとも、2025 年度が同＋16.3％と、価
格転嫁の浸透などを背景に想定を大きく上回って着地したため、その反動減という側面も大きい。コロナ禍前と
の比較では、引き続き高めの水準を維持している。

3 
企業が高水準の収益を見込むもとで、関西企業は積極的な設備投資姿勢を維持している。日本銀行大
阪支店「短観」６月調査による2026年度の設備投資計画（全産業・全規模）は前年度比＋9.7％と、過
去（2010～2024 年度）の平均伸び率を上回る水準にある。機械設備や建設資材の価格上昇などから名
目の投資額が押し上げられている点は割り引く必要があるものの、中東情勢の緊迫化が企業の設備投資マイ
ンドに与える影響は今のところ限定的にとどまっている。ソフトウエア投資も前年度比＋5.1％と増勢が続いてお
り、効率化や省力化を目的とした企業の積極的な投資姿勢に変化はみられない。

（３） 雇用・所得動向 
関西の雇用環境は、人手不足を背景に良好な状態が続いている。雇用関連指標をみると、有効求人倍率
は１倍を超え、完全失業率（季節調整値）は 2％台後半の低位で推移している。雇用者数は高めの水準
を保っている。 
所得面をみると、府県別「毎月勤労統計調査」から推計した４～６月期の関西の一人当たり現金給与総
額は前年同期比＋4.3％と、前期に比べてプラス幅が拡大した。今年の春闘における高い賃上げ水準での妥
結や、夏季賞与の増加（大阪府内企業を対象とする調査では前年比＋2.5％）などを反映し、賃金は高め

の伸びとなった。雇用者報酬を実質ベースでみると、物
価高による下押し圧力の緩和も相まって、4～６月期
は前年同期比＋３％超の増加となった。もっとも、春
先以降の原油高の影響がラグを伴って波及すると予想
されるほか、中東情勢の混乱が長期化するなか、原油
価格は依然として高水準にある。先行き、物価の伸び
は再び拡大する公算が大きく、実質所得には下押し圧
力が強い状況が続く見込みである。

（４） 消費動向 
関西の小売販売額をみると、スーパー、コンビニエンスストア、百貨店の販売額は緩やかに増加している。 
需要側統計（家計調査）をみると、実質ベースで見た消費支出は、4 月に税負担の軽減が実施された自
動車購入の増加などによる一時的な押し上げがみられたものの、均してみれば、物価高への警戒感が根強いも
とで、回復の勢いは鈍い。 
消費者マインドは、中東情勢の混乱直後の大幅
な悪化から改善傾向にあるものの、依然として悪化
前の水準を回復していない。とりわけ、大幅な落ち込
みがみられた「暮らし向き」や「耐久消費財の買い時
判断」の改善が遅れている。地政学リスクの長期化を
背景とした物価高への根強い警戒感が消費者マイン
ドの重石となっている。 
先行きの個人消費は、賃上げの持続が下支えに
寄与するものの、消費者物価の伸びが再び拡大する
もとで、回復ペースは緩慢にとどまる公算が大きい。

（５）住宅・公共投資動向 
関西の新設住宅着工戸数は、2025 年
４月の法改正を受けて上下に大きく振れた
ものの、 その後は落ち着きを取り戻し、概ね
法改正の影響を受ける前の水準に復してい
る。住宅需要は、金利の上昇や建築費の高
騰などによる下押し圧力が強いものの、将来
の資産価値への期待や、金利・建築費の先
高観を背景とした需要の高まりが下支えとな
り、大幅な落ち込みには至っていない。

2026 年７月の公共工事請負金額は前
年同月比＋15.0％となった。国土強靱化や
インフラ更新需要のほか、建設コストの上昇な
どを背景に、概ね前年を上回るペースで推移
している。

（６）倒産件数 
関西の企業倒産件数は、振れを伴いながらも、均してみるとコロナ禍前を上回る水準で推移している。原材
料費の高騰や人件費の上昇などに加え、金利上昇局面における資金繰りの悪化が懸念される。金利の上昇
によって新たに赤字に陥る中小企業の割合は全国よりも関西の方が大きいとの試算もあり、倒産件数は高止ま
りする可能性が高い。

（７）観光動向 
関西の外国人延べ宿泊者数は、コロナ禍前を上回って推移している。中国人旅行者の落ち込みが続いてい
るものの、その他の国・地域からの旅行者が全体を下支えしている。もっとも、足元ではその他の国・地域からの
旅行者も伸びが鈍化しており、インバウンド需要は全体として頭打ち感が強い。日本人の延べ宿泊者数につい
ても、宿泊料の上昇などを背景に、足元でコロナ禍前の水準を割り込んでいる。

３．トピック：AI 関連需要の拡大と関西の製造業生産 
AI 関連需要が拡大するなか、関西の半導体製造装置の生産は、振れを伴いながらも均してみれば全国よ
り大きく増加している。加えて、AI 関連需要は非鉄金属やゴム製品など川中、川上分野の素材産業にも広く
波及している。こうした動きを背景に、関連産業では生産能力の増強を目的とした設備投資を活発化させる動
きがみられる。 
一方、関西の電子部品・デバイスの生産は低迷が続いており、持ち直しに転じているわが国全体の動きと乖
離が生じている。この背景には、①公的支援を受けた次世代半導体の大規模生産拠点が九州や東北、中部
などで本格稼働していること、②関西に本社を置く電子部品・デバイス企業であっても製造拠点が関西に集中
しているわけではなく、国内各地に分散していること、があげられる。

### Page 7

7 
ただし、関西の電子部品・デバイス企業は、高付加価値化の源泉となる研究開発機能を関西圏に集約して
いると推測される。実際、関西の電子部品・デバイス・電子回路製造業の就業者数は九州や中部と同水準な
がら、研究者・技術者の割合は関東に次いで高水準となっている。 
したがって、関西の電子部品・デバイス生産の低迷は、競争力の弱さを直ちに意味するものではなく、各社が
生産拠点と研究開発拠点の地域ごとの役割分担を踏まえて、最適な拠点配置を進めた結果と捉えるべきであ
ろう。

聯發科 2454 TT ( 電子上游–IC 設計 ) 
件 ) 
金麟化龍，再登巔峰 
投資與評價 
1. ASIC 貢獻優於預期、以及 smart edge platform 挹注下，抵銷手機下
滑影響，預估 2026 年營收將年增 14.2%。 
2. 預估 ASIC 貢獻將於 2027 更加驚豔，上修營收貢獻至 5000 億台幣水
準，手機則呈現回溫態勢，預估 2027 年營收年增 83.3%。 
3. 預估聯發科 2026/2027 年 EPS 為 73.85/171.17 元，考量 ASIC 成長
性，AI 能見度高且延續性佳，以歷史本益比區間中間偏上位置 28 倍為
評價基礎，目標價 4800 元，維持買進投資評等。 
重點更新 
AI 成長優於預期，抵銷手機疲弱影響，上修 2026 年營收至年增 14.2% 
手機業務在 3Q26 年季減 1%，聯發科轉向高端機種來因應原物料成本上升
問題，在客戶陸續推出搭載聯發科 SoC 的新 AI 旗艦手機下，研究部修正預
估手機整年衰退約 16.2%。smart edge platforms 今年主要受惠於 AI 平板 、
AI PC 及車用座艙等專案量產，預估整年營收年增 13.5%，power IC 業務受
惠資料中心相關業務市占提升，研究部上修整年成長約為 5%。今年 AI ASIC
研究部預估貢獻從 20.8 億美元上修至 25.1 億美元營收，佔整體營收 11.4%
客戶需求優於預期，且獲得供應鏈的支援。整體而言，預估 2026 年營收隨
著在 AI ASIC 以及 smart edge platforms 貢獻下，可抵銷手機下滑影響，並
上修成長幅度，營收預估年增 14.2%，達 6804.5 億元，毛利率因成本上升以
及 ASIC 毛利較低，略為下滑至 46.2%。 
AI ASIC 迎來爆發期，算力需求令人驚艷，上修 2027 營收年增 83.3% 
AI ASIC 強進需求將延續至 2027 年整年，而根據調研下一代 ASIC 產品不管
在規模與定價皆高於第一個專案，推估將於 1Q28 進入量產 ，2027 年來自資
料中心 ASIC 營收上修至貢獻超過 5000 億台幣規模(約多 1200 億元)，營收
佔比將來到四成水準，佔比高於先前約 6%。而隨著 AI 導入生活以及相關應
用需求的增長下，算力及高效連結雲端為必備功能，手機部分 在低基期下預
估將成長約 23%。而在邊緣 AI 部分，隨著代理 AI 趨勢，邊緣設備迎來結構
性增長，預估維持高速成長態勢。研究部預估 2027 年營收隨著在 AI ASIC 爆
發及手機回溫下，營收將年增83.3%，突破一兆大關，並且毛利率雖因 ASIC
占比較高下略有稀釋，但 ASIC 營業利益率較高，整體提升至 23.17%。

資料來源：台灣指數公司，國泰證期研究部整理

### Page 2

訪談內容 
需求毫無疑慮，成長順勢而起 
手機方面，AI 落地需求以及作為日常生活必需品，研究部認為代理 AI 創新加速發展，公司正與
Android 生態系合作，推動整合至 SoC，並看好其應用延伸至 wearable、mobile、IoT、PC 與
automotive。

在 smart edge platforms 方面，搭載 N1X 的 NVIDIA RTX Spark AI PC 於 10 月中旬透過主要
PC OEM 夥伴上市，結合 NVIDIA GPU 與聯發科 SoC，支援裝置端代理式 AI。Dimensity 9500
搭載於 Samsung Galaxy T ab S12系列 AI 平板，為 Samsung AI 平板連續第三代採用聯發科旗
艦 SoC。

AI ASIC 方面，第二款 AI 加速器封裝方案執行進展良好，目標較前代提高數倍每瓦效能並降低總
持有成本，部分晶粒已完成 tape-out，較大且複雜的晶粒仍依時程推進，將與美國 IDM 封裝夥
伴及台、日基板供應商合作改善良率，並爭取需求上行所需產能，研究部預估 1Q28 進入量產。

ASIC 亦已取得不同於既有主要客戶的第二家資料中心 ASIC 客戶，專案已啟動，預計2028 年開
始量產及貢獻營收， 另外資料中心業務將從晶片延伸至系統與機櫃 ， 透過NVIDIA NVLink Fusion
將客製化 XPU 接入 NVLink 生態系，並支援 UALink，降低部署複雜度、縮短上市與營收實現時
間，並持續推進高速互連、先進封裝與 CPO，448Gbps SerDes 已完成 tape-out，後續進行量
測、驗證及互通性開發，服務更廣泛客戶。

表一、聯發科 2027 年修正預估與前次預估比較

資料來源：國泰證期研究部預估

### Page 3

聯發科 ESG 政策 
聯發科主要從事 IC 設計 ， 產業類別歸類在半導體行業中 ， 依據專門為投資人設計的永續揭露框架 SASB
準則，半導體行業關注重點為下列幾項：溫室氣體排放、能源管理、水和廢水管理、廢棄物和危險材料
管理、員工健康與安全、員工多元化和包容性、產品設計與生命週期管理、材料採購與效率。由於聯發
科為無廠 IC 設計公司，研究部將篇幅著重在產品設計面，並因員工對於IC 設計公司而言是相當重要的
資產，故員工相關議題也會著重於下描述，其餘溫室氣體排放及水和廢水管理等則於後簡述。

1. 員工健康與安全： 
為確保承攬商施工安全管理 ， 對於入場施工之人員均辦理職業安全衛生講習 ， 進行危害告知及宣導安全
施工規範，以預防職業災害發生。2022 年參與安全講習之承攬商共有 793 人並達成年度零災害，零
意外、零損失之目標。

圖 1 員工失能、職業病、缺席比率

資料來源:聯發科、國泰證期研究部整理

2. 員工多元化和包容性： 
聯發科技重視研發創新及國際化營運，視全球人才布局為技術研發的驅動力。截至  2022 年年底，聯
發科技員工總人數為 18,993 人。為有效運籌全球研發能量，聯發科技在全球設有 32 個辦公據點，
海外人員數占總人數約 35.4%。期待透過全球人才交流合作，加速產品開發時程與技術支援即時性。

圖 2 員工年齡架構 
資料來源:聯發科、國泰證期研究部整理

### Page 4

3. 溫室氣體排放： 
聯發科技能資源使用所排放之溫室氣體，排放來源包含外購電力、公用設備、鍋爐、冷卻水塔、冰水主
機等，主要排放源為範疇二的外購電力占整體 93.27%。在公司營運規模持續成長的表現下，致力提高
能源使用效率與規劃再生能源使用，以降低公司營運所產生的溫室氣體排放量。2022 年度主要減量作
為包含持續提升資料中心之能效，及完成既有辦公室據點照明更新為 LED 等，以提高用電效率。

圖 3 2022 年溫室氣體排放情形

資料來源:聯發科、國泰證期研究部整理

4. 水和廢水管理： 
聯發科技之辦公室取水來自各區自來水廠供水，因今年度員工人數增加，且新建置 5 個辦公據點以及 
IT 資料中心之擴充 ， 共取用 485,629 公噸 （約 486 百萬公升） 水量 ， 其取水量相較於前一年增加 27%，
也因此無法達成 2021 年所設定之目標 5%，雖總用水量提升，聯發科技持續進行水資源項目改善計
畫，平均節水比例達 3.7%，未來將持續搭配進行各項用水減量措施，達成每年減少用水 5% 的目標。
廢水主要為生活污水 ， 依各區納管統一由廢水處理廠處理後排放至自然水體 ， 不會對當地環境產生影響
與衝擊，2022 年排放 202,792 公噸（約 203 百萬公升）廢水，其廢水量相較於前一年增加12%，與
前述用水量增加之原因相同。

圖 4 用水量、節水量與節水比例圖  
資料來源:聯發科、國泰證期研究部整理

市場投資展望
《臉書 Muse 點燃追趕行情，谷歌困於模型商品化
與旗艦空窗》
臉書代理式 AI 產品 Muse 迅速爆紅，推動股價單日
創 13 個月來最佳表現。市場開始將臉書從「AI 資
本支出大戶」重新定位為「具備自有 AI 爆款產品的
平台」。月底臉書乘勝進軍企業 AI 平台。然而，AI
代理競爭仍在初期 ，臉書雖挾分發優勢 ，但變現仍
待觀察。谷歌則在法務與搜尋基本面接連傳出利多 。
然而，OpenAI 與 Anthropic 接連大砍 API 價格導
致 AI 推理商品化疑慮，加上旗艦模型 Gemini 4 仍

遲遲未推出 ，谷歌股價因此持續承壓 。資本支出強
度攀升導致的 ROIC 走弱的核心敘事尚未見到反轉 ，
因此通訊服務仍維持中性看待。
• 9 月臉書推出面向消費端的 AI
代理 Muse，正面回應市場持
續關注的變現疑問。
• 臉書近期也成立企業 AI 平台，
將代理人技術延伸至企業端。
• 臉書成功回應市場對臉書有無
AI 應用產品的疑慮。但投入回
收成效仍待進一步驗證。
• 谷歌整合式廣告業務營運未受
結構性衝擊，搜尋有自 AI 競
爭者收復失土的跡象。
• 但谷歌在 AI 模型層仍面臨 AI
兩大壓力，不利市場信心。
1
資產 4Q26 看法 投資展望
通訊服務 中立 通訊服務主成分臉書近期推出消費端 AI 代理 Muse 與企業 AI 平台，
成功回應市場對於「臉書有沒有 AI 產品」的疑問，然後續 AI 投資變
現結果仍待逐步驗證。谷歌流失在廣告與搜尋業務迎來好消息，然在
AI 模型端面臨雙重逆風，不利市場信心。本行預期市場仍將聚焦檢視
資本支出效率與變現進度。靜候 10 月財報結果，維持中立評等。

重點摘要
零售客流量表
• 9 月臉書推出面向消費端的 AI
代理 Muse，正面回應市場持
續關注的變現疑問。

2
《臉書 AI 投資變現路徑初具雛形，消費端與企業端
同步開展》
9 月臉書推出面向消費端的 AI 代理 Muse，正面回
應市場持續關注的「臉書能否以自有 AI 產品在 40
億用戶基礎上變現」的疑問。Muse 是能代替用戶
執行比價、訂購、換約等具體任務的個人代理人。
上市後迅速登上美國 App Store 與 Google Play 下
載榜首，印證臉書既有社群入口與用戶關係在 AI 產

品分發上的結構性優勢。Muse 雖為臉書打開訂閱、
交易與導購分潤、廣告業務強化三條潛在變現路徑，
但其成敗仍取決於 Muse 能否延續上線初期的動能、
擴展至美國以外市場，並深入用戶日常使用情境。
同時，Muse 的代理能力正引發平台間的摩擦。亞
馬遜隨即封鎖其代理購物功能，拒絕第三方代理人
直接在其平台上替用戶下單，銀行、保險、旅遊等
仰賴消費者惰性賺取溢價的產業也可能採取類似的
防禦措施，交易分潤路徑能否成立，將取決於臉書
與既有平台的合作或議價能力。
緊接在 Muse 之後，臉書近期也宣布成立企業 AI 平
台，並延攬 MongoDB 執行長 CJ Desai 領導此一新
事業，以圖將代理人技術從消費端延伸至企業端。
臉書在企業市場的最大資產是全球大量中小企業已
用來與客戶溝通的 WhatsApp。在 WhatsApp 基礎
上衍生的自動處理詢價、下單、售後客服的企業 AI
代理應用具有相當的想像空間。不過，本行認為臉
書企業平台執行的不確定性明顯高於 Muse。主要
仍是考量到中小企業的數據品質、客戶信任與資安

風險與消費端截然不同。臉書過去缺乏企業銷售團
隊與服務紀錄，從零建立企業級通路需要時間與額
外投入，或將加重臉書的資本與費用負擔。
因此，本行認為 Muse 僅回應了市場對臉書 AI 投資
變現的第一個問題「臉書有沒有 AI 產品」。第二個
問題「投入何時能回收」，仍有待後續進展驗證。
